--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score increases for every **stationary** robot → robots should **move**
- After switch time: score increases for every **moving** robot → robots should **stop**
- Switch is signaled by a **GREEN wall** lighting up
- The robot can detect green via condition C=7 (perceives colour, colour 2=green)

**Optimal strategy:**
1. **State 0**: Move (Exploration) — before the switch, keep moving to avoid penalty
2. **State 1**: Stop — after switch, stop to avoid penalty
3. **Transition**: When robot sees GREEN (C=7, l=2), switch from moving to stopping

The key insight: robots should move by default, and when they perceive green (the lit wall), switch to stopping.

**Design considerations:**
- Need to detect green wall appearance → C=7 with colour 2 (green)
- After stopping, might want to stay stopped unless... green disappears? But green stays on, so once stopped, stay stopped.
- Could also add a random-probability fallback to restart exploration if somehow isolated
- 2-state machines are simplest: Explore → Stop on green detection
- 3-state machines can add nuance: e.g., attraction phase, then stop

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **Best candidate** (elitism placeholder - no prior best, so design the strongest): 2-state, explore → stop on green
2-4: Variants with different exploration rates and probabilities
5-7: 3-state machines with attraction/repulsion
8-10: Variants with multiple transitions
11-14: More exploration

FSM designs:

- **2-state core**: S0=Exploration(rwm), S1=Stop; S0→S1 on green(C=7,l=2,p=0.9); S1→S0 on no-green? No, once green appears it stays. Keep S1 with low prob back to S0.
- For S1→S0: use C=5 with very low probability (in case green wasn't seen, robot is stuck stopped early)

Wait, S1 has transitions back to S0 - that means after seeing green and stopping, it could go back to exploring. That's bad. Let me reconsider...

Actually once green appears (after switch time), we want to stay stopped. A very small probability of returning to exploration is okay as a failsafe for pre-switch false positives. But p=0.005 should be fine.

Let me also think about: the C=5 (random probability) transition from Stop back to Explore - this allows robots that stopped too early (before the switch, due to not seeing green yet) to restart. But actually before the switch there's no green, so they won't stop at all if the only trigger is C=7 green. So this is fine.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The mission requires robots to:
- **Move** before the switch time (to avoid penalty for being stationary)
- **Stop** after the switch time (to avoid penalty for being moving)
- The switch is signaled by a **GREEN wall** lighting up (detectable via C=7, colour 2=green)

The core strategy is clear: **Explore by default, Stop when green is perceived.**

Since I have no feedback on scores from rounds 1-2 (just told rounds completed), I'll design the best candidates I can reason about:

**Key design decisions:**
1. State 0 = Exploration (moving), State 1 = Stop
2. Transition from Explore→Stop triggered by seeing green (C=7, l=2, high probability)
3. Stop state should be stable - only small random probability of returning to explore (as insurance if robot stopped before switch)
4. The probability of transitioning on green detection should be high (0.9-1.0) but not so high that noise causes issues
5. Exploration rate (rwm) affects how well robots spread out - moderate values (10-30) are good

**Survival/Elitism:** I'll include the strongest candidates from my reasoning.

**Variants to try:**
- Different rwm values (5, 10, 20, 30, 50)
- Different green-detection probabilities (0.8, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0)
- Multi-state machines with intermediate attraction state
- GoToColor(green) as exploration behavior (actively seek the green wall to detect it faster)
- Multiple transitions mixing conditions

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 3 rounds, I haven't received score feedback, so I'll reason from first principles and diversify my designs.

**Core strategy recap:**
- Before switch: robots must MOVE (stationary robots penalized)
- After switch: robots must STOP (moving robots penalized)
- Switch signal: GREEN wall lights up → detectable via C=7 (colour 2=green)

**Key insight I may be missing:** The camera on e-pucks has limited field of view. Not all robots will see the green wall at the same time. Robots near the wall will see it first, others may not see it at all if they're facing away. 

**Improved strategies:**
1. **Signal propagation**: Robots that see green could change LED color (e.g., to green/cyan), allowing other robots to detect the signal via C=7 with that LED color. This creates a cascade effect.
2. **GoToColor(green)**: After seeing green, move toward it briefly then stop - but this increases movement after switch, bad.
3. **Better**: Use robot LEDs to propagate the green signal. State 0: Explore (LED off), State 1: Stop + emit green LED (cle=2), others can detect green from neighbors.

**Signal cascade design:**
- S0: Explore (LED=0), transition to S1 on seeing green (C=7, l=2)
- S1: Stop (LED=2=green), no transitions back
- Other robots in S0 see green LEDs from stopped neighbors → also transition to S1

Wait, but color 2 = green for LEDs. The wall also emits green. So C=7 with l=2 would detect BOTH the wall AND green LEDs on other robots. This is perfect for cascade signaling!

**This is likely the key improvement** - stopped robots emit green LEDs to signal others.

Let me design machines with this cascade mechanism:

**Design A (cascade, 2-state):**
- S0: Explore, LED=0, transition on seeing green (l=2) → S1
- S1: Stop, LED=2(green), tiny prob back to S0

**Design B (cascade, 3-state):**
- S0: Explore fast, LED=0, on green → S1
- S1: Stop, LED=2, tiny prob → S0
- Could add intermediate state

**Design C (GoToColor then stop):**
- S0: Explore, LED=0, on green → S1
- S1: GoToColor(green) briefly to confirm → S2 on high neighbor count
- S2: Stop, LED=2

Actually GoToColor after switch increases score. Better to just stop immediately.

**Key variants to include:**
1. Cascade (explore → stop+green LED) - multiple rwm values
2. Non-cascade (explore → stop, no LED)  
3. 3-state with attraction intermediate
4. Different detection probabilities
5. Repulsion-based exploration (spreads robots better for green detection)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Critical correction:** LED colours allowed are {0, 4, 5, 6} only (0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). I was wrongly using 2 (green) for LEDs. I need to use a different colour for the cascade signal - let's use **cyan (6)** for stopped robots, so other robots can detect cyan via C=7 with l=6.

**Revised cascade strategy:**
- S0: Explore (LED=0), transition to S1 when perceiving green (C=7, l=2) - the wall
- S1: Stop (LED=6=cyan), so neighbours can detect cyan and also stop
- Other robots: also transition on seeing cyan (C=7, l=6) from stopped neighbours

This creates a propagation wave: wall turns green → nearby robots see green → stop and emit cyan → further robots see cyan → stop.

**Available colours for detection (C=7):**
- l=2: green (the wall signal)
- l=6: cyan (our stopped-robot signal)

**Design families:**
1. **2-state cascade**: Explore → Stop(cyan LED) on green wall OR cyan neighbours
2. **2-state simple**: Explore → Stop on green only
3. **3-state**: Explore → Stop(cyan) with attraction intermediate
4. **Repulsion-based exploration**: Spread robots out to maximize coverage for green detection

Let me be careful with all parameters this time.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error says I'm still using `--cle1 2` somewhere, but looking at my Round 5 answer I used `--cle1 6`. The error message must be referring to something persistent from earlier rounds. I'll be extra careful: **LED colours must be from {0, 4, 5, 6} only**.

**Strategy review:**
- Core strategy is sound: Move before switch, Stop after switch (when green wall appears)
- Cascade idea: Stopped robots emit cyan (6) LED, others detect cyan and also stop
- The wall emits green (detectable as colour 2 via C=7)
- Our stopped robots emit cyan (6), detectable as colour 6 via C=7

**Improvements to consider:**
1. The cascade mechanism is theoretically strong - robots near the wall stop first, emit cyan, propagating the stop signal
2. I should vary the exploration behaviour more (repulsion spreads robots better for coverage)
3. Consider that a robot stopping early (by random chance) is costly before switch - keep random-stop probabilities very low
4. The `--n1x0` in Stop state going back to Explore should have very low probability

Let me also consider: what if all robots just move randomly and stop only on green detection? The issue is robots far from the green wall may never see it. The cascade with cyan LEDs solves this. Let me focus on well-tuned cascade machines.

**All LED values will be strictly from {0, 4, 5, 6}:**
- 0 = off (exploring state)
- 6 = cyan (stopped state - signal for cascade)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error keeps appearing about `--cle1 2`, but I haven't used that value in rounds 5 or 6. The error system may be reporting cumulative issues. I need to be absolutely certain every `--cle<I>` value is in {0, 4, 5, 6}.

**Let me audit my designs carefully:**
- Exploring state: LED = 0 (off) ✓
- Stopped state: LED = 6 (cyan) ✓
- Never use LED values 1, 2, 3 ✓

**Strategy assessment after 6 rounds:**

The core logic is correct:
- Move before green wall appears
- Stop when green wall (or cyan-LED neighbours) detected
- Cascade: stopped robots emit cyan (6), propagating stop signal

**What might be suboptimal:**
1. The random-return probability from Stop→Explore might be too high, causing robots to restart moving after switch
2. Maybe some robots never detect green/cyan if spread too far apart - repulsion-based exploration might hurt cascade
3. The `--p1x0` (return probability) should be extremely small (0.001 or less) to keep stopped robots stopped

**Key refinements for round 7:**
- Keep return probability from Stop very low (0.001)
- Try machines with NO return transition from Stop (but rules require at least 1 transition per state)
- Emphasize the cascade (green + cyan detection) as primary mechanism
- Add variety in exploration behaviour
- Try attraction-based movement to keep robots clustered (better cascade range)

**13 machines:**
- 4 elitism/exploitation: best cascade designs with small tweaks
- 5 exploitation: parameter variations
- 4 exploration: different topologies

## Reasoning

The error about `--cle1 2` keeps appearing. Let me think carefully: could this be coming from some other parameter that I'm confusing? Let me re-read the rules:

`--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}

So for state 1, `--cle1` must be 0, 4, 5, or 6. I've been using 6 (cyan) which IS valid. But the error persists. 

Wait - could the issue be with `--clr<I>` (the colour parameter for GoToColor/GoAwayColor behaviors)? No, that's different.

Could it be that colour `2` in `--l<I>x<K>` (the colour to look for in C=7) is being confused? No, C=7 uses `--l<I>x<K>` not `--cle<I>`.

Let me check: is there any place I wrote `--cle1 2`? Looking at my round 6 answer... I see `--cle1 6` everywhere. Perhaps the error system is showing a lag from earlier rounds.

**I will proceed with exactly the same valid values and be maximally careful. I'll double-check every single `--cle` parameter.**

**Strategy update:**
Given 7 rounds of consistent strategy, let me also try some genuinely different approaches:
1. What if the green wall detection is unreliable at distance? Maybe robots should use attraction to cluster first, then one robot near wall triggers cascade
2. What if we try a 4-state machine with more sophisticated signaling?
3. What if we try GoToColor(green) as the initial state to actively seek the wall?

Actually, GoToColor(green) before the switch would mean robots cluster near the wall, then when switch happens they all see green immediately and stop. But GoToColor also moves the robot (adding score before switch only if stationary is penalized - wait, moving is GOOD before switch). So GoToColor(green) is valid as the pre-switch behavior, and when they're near the wall they'll detect it immediately.

**New idea**: State 0 = GoToColor(green, clr=2), which actively seeks the green wall. Before switch, no green exists so robots just wander toward where green would be (or explore randomly?). Actually GoToColor would have no target before the switch, so it might behave like exploration. After switch, green appears and GoToColor drives robots toward the wall - but that's moving (bad after switch). 

Actually the better interpretation: use Exploration before switch, then stop after. The cascade mechanism (cyan LEDs) is the best propagation approach.

**Let me also try:** magenta (5) LED instead of cyan for the stopped state, to check if colour 6 detection is reliable. And try yellow (4).

**Most important**: make sure every `--cle<I>` is in {0, 4, 5, 6}.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The persistent error about `--cle1 2` is puzzling since I haven't used that value recently. Let me reconsider: could this error be triggered by something else entirely? 

Wait - I just realized: in my 4-state machine from round 8, state indices go 0,1,2,3. When state 2 has transitions, `--n2x0` destination D is indexed among OTHER states. With 4 states, leaving state 2, others are [0,1,3], so D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state3. Let me check: `--n2x0 0` targets state 0, `--n2x1 1` targets state 1. That seems fine.

But wait - could the error come from the **colour parameter** `--clr<I>` for GoToColor/GoAwayColor? No, I haven't used those recently.

Let me re-examine: `--l0x1 6` — this is the colour to look for in C=7 condition. Is 6 valid for `--l<I>x<K>`? The spec says colours {1,2,3,4,5,6}. Yes, 6 is valid.

**Actually**, I wonder if the simulator is detecting `--cle1 6` as problematic because of some other parsing issue, OR if the error message is stale/cumulative from earlier rounds. Since I cannot debug the simulator directly, I'll just make absolutely sure every `--cle` parameter is valid and try slightly different machine structures.

**I'll also try a fundamentally different approach**: What if robots use **yellow (4)** LEDs when stopped, and detect yellow from neighbors? This avoids any potential issue with cyan (6).

Also, let me reconsider the whole design space. After 8 rounds, the optimiser should have found good parameters. Let me focus on:

1. The cleanest possible 2-state cascade machines (most reliable)
2. Avoid complex 4-state machines that might have parsing errors
3. Use yellow (4) as alternative stopped-robot signal
4. Keep `--p1x0` (return from stop) extremely small

**Careful parameter audit for every machine I write:**
- `--cle0`: must be in {0,4,5,6} → using 0 ✓
- `--cle1`: must be in {0,4,5,6} → using 4, 5, or 6 ✓
- `--cle2`: must be in {0,4,5,6} → using 0 ✓
- `--l<I>x<K>`: colour to detect, must be in {1,2,3,4,5,6} → using 2 (green wall) or 6 (cyan) ✓

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The error about `--cle1 2` keeps appearing despite me not using that value in recent rounds. I need to think about this differently.

**Could the issue be with `--clr<I>`?** For GoToColor (B=8) and GoAwayColor (B=9), the parameter is `--clr<I>` with values {1,2,3,4,5,6}. That's different from `--cle<I>`.

**Could the issue be with state numbering?** With 2 states (0 and 1), `--cle1` refers to state 1's LED. Let me make sure I haven't accidentally written something like `--cle1 2` anywhere.

Actually, I wonder if the error is being triggered by the **`--l<I>x<K>`** parameter where I use `l=2` for detecting green. But that's the colour-to-detect parameter for C=7, not the LED colour. The spec says `--l<I>x<K>` takes values {1,2,3,4,5,6} which is different from `--cle<I>` values {0,4,5,6}.

Wait - I just realized something. What if in my 3-state machines, when I write `--cle2 0`, this is state 2's LED = 0 which is valid. But what if there's a parsing conflict where `--cle1` is being read as having value from the next token?

**Let me try a completely clean approach**: Only use `--cle0 0` and `--cle1 0` (LED off for all states) to eliminate any possible LED-colour issues. The cascade mechanism through LED colours may not be working anyway if there's a parsing error.

**Alternative strategy without LED cascade:**
- Just use direct green-wall detection (C=7, l=2) to stop
- Robots near wall stop, others keep moving (accepting some post-switch movement penalty for distant robots)
- This is simpler and avoids potential LED issues

**For this final round, I'll:**
1. Use only LED=0 for all states to be safe
2. Focus purely on green-wall detection
3. Try varying exploration styles and detection probabilities
4. Keep return probability from Stop extremely low

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
